## AI Research

### [TeX was invented to typeset math but is now used for reasoning](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CFKyfZm5JBYZQ8aG6/tex-was-invented-to-typeset-math-but-is-now-used-for)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'll keep this short, since it's a simple observation that I haven't seen anybody else make, about the way computer systems do math.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you ask a language model to do a multi-step math problem (let's take GLM-5.3 as an example because you can see the entire CoT — nothing up its sleeve), you might see something like this:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We need to evaluate the integral $\int_0^\infty \frac{x^3}{e^x - 1} dx$.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The standard approach: Use the geometric series expansion. We have $\frac{1}{e^x - 1} = \frac{e^{-x}}{1 - e^{-x}} = \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} e^{-nx}$ for $x &gt; 0$.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So the integral becomes:</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">$$\int_0^\infty x^3 \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} e^{-nx} dx = \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \int_0^\infty x^3 e^{-nx} dx$$</span></p></pre><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are all these symbols like </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">\int</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">\infty</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">\frac</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...? They're TeX of course!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Donald Knuth created TeX to </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">typeset</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> math, you know, for </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">display</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It had nothing to do with the actual computations, which would either be done with pencil and paper,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefozx6s9uf93m"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnozx6s9uf93m">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or else with Mathematica or Maple or something, which work completely differently. If you'd asked Knuth in the 1980s about doing algebra in TeX he'd have looked at you very funny because the idea doesn't make sense.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxr4sgq7gnqg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxr4sgq7gnqg">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then, generative language models were trained on corpora including many TeX/LaTeX documents and learned how TeX works (and more importantly the mathematical meaning of the symbols).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, nowadays when an LLM solves a math problem, it will very often use TeX for the </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intermediate steps</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the math problem, like it's actually manipulating the TeX symbols in order to </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reason</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We can tell it's using it this way because it happens in the CoT, which for many AI products is never rendered/displayed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So we ended up in a world where the most powerful mathematical problem solving agents (e.g. the ones behind the recently claimed Navier-Stokes solution) reason using TeX.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8xiino8kfhq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8xiino8kfhq">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Knuth has </span><a href="https://www-cs-faculty.stanford.edu/~knuth/papers/claude-cycles.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">written briefly about generative AI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but I don't think he's ever commented on this unexpected development... and I'm not sure if he's even aware that Claude's solution to his own problem was very plausibly produced by Claude manipulating and "thinking in" TeX.)</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnozx6s9uf93m"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefozx6s9uf93m">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, pencil-and-paper computation uses the same notation that TeX encodes, since TeX didn't invent ∫, or "sqrt" for √. But the point is that TeX became the dominant </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">plain-text</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1D</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> encoding of this traditional 2D math notation, and so LLMs learned to use it... for thinking, not just typesetting.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxr4sgq7gnqg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxr4sgq7gnqg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, he might actually have said "oh sure, you mean like macros and stuff? like how TeX is Turing-complete?" and then when you clarified that you meant a non-deterministic system that doesn't even run a TeX engine at all but still directly manipulates the TeX symbols in order to do algebra... then he would have looked at you </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">really</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> funny.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8xiino8kfhq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8xiino8kfhq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They also published a formal Lean proof, so you might say that the strongest agents reason in TeX but </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">verify</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in Lean. "TeX for thinking, Lean for checking."</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CFKyfZm5JBYZQ8aG6/tex-was-invented-to-typeset-math-but-is-now-used-for#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The likely outcome of an AI pause is that we unpause too early and everyone dies](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PgbAnqdqdJcrS2Gsy/the-likely-outcome-of-an-ai-pause-is-that-we-unpause-too)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><em>Cross-posted from <a href="https://mdickens.me/2026/09/28/unpause_too_early/">my website</a>.</em></p>
<p>As of a few months ago, I had this simplified mental model where either AI developers race ahead and kill everyone, or we coordinate a pause and things go okay. But my old mental model underrated the likely possibility that we get a global pause on AI, solve a problem that looks superficially like the alignment problem, resume scaling, and then proceed with building a misaligned superintelligence that kills everyone.</p>
<p>A lot of people have become more concerned about misalignment recently. This seems driven by the fact that current AI models are visibly misaligned. But ASI misalignment is a whole different ball game. The primary danger comes from AI that's smarter than people, and smart enough to conceal any evidence of misalignment.</p>
<p>Whatever group of people makes the decision to unpause, I'm worried that they won't understand the difference between visible and actual misalignment, and they will unpause too early.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/PgbAnqdqdJcrS2Gsy/mwwneuw06gwbbppfxsy3" /></p>
<p><em>source: <a href="https://www.reddit.com/r/OpenAI/comments/1nu8zmn/anthropic_sonnet_45_recognized_many_of_our/">MetaKnowing on reddit.</a> This meme is almost a year old but it's only gotten more relevant since then.</em></p>
<p>Case in point: AI companies keep calling their new models "our most aligned model ever!" when what they actually mean is "gets the best scores on alignment benchmarks ever!" First, alignment benchmarks do not actually test alignment. We don't <em>know</em> how to test for alignment. Second, GPT-4 never <a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/">hacked into Hugging Face</a> or <a href="https://collusion.wiki/">took over a German wiki for its own purposes</a>. GPT-4 wasn't smart enough to do that, but if we're talking about <em>demonstrated evidence of misalignment</em>, then we have stronger evidence about OpenAI's 2026 internal model than about GPT-4.</p>
<h2>If we unpause when the legible problems are solved, we die</h2>
<p>Suppose we get a pause. Researchers spend years working on <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PMc65HgRFvBimEpmJ/legible-vs-illegible-ai-safety-problems">legible safety problems</a>—problems that company leaders and policy-makers can see and understand, and therefore won't unpause until they're solved. Eventually, all the legible problems are solved. Key decision-makers conclude that the <em>whole</em> problem is solved, and lift the pause on ASI development. Many illegible problems remain unsolved, but the detectable signs of misalignment are all gone. Post-pause AI will be smarter than the smartest pre-pause AI,<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> which means it's probably smart enough to strategically conceal misalignment. That means there are no more warning signs. We never get detectable evidence of misalignment; we cede control of everything to AI; and eventually we die.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>There are many people with a good understanding of the conceptual difficulties in aligning superintelligence. Some names that come to mind are Eliezer Yudkowsky, Wei Dai, and John Wentworth.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span> (Probably, many people reading this post fall into that category.) Those people wouldn't make the mistake of confusing alignment with observable alignment. Unfortunately, I do not expect these people to be key decision-makers, and I do not expect key decision-makers to understand the relevant problems.</p>
<h2>A pause alone doesn't get us to a science of alignment</h2>
<p><a href="https://ai-2040.com/">AI 2040: Plan A</a> has a vision where the world develops a "science of alignment".<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span> I sure hope that happens, and I encourage efforts to push things in that direction, but we don't seem on track to get a science of alignment even in the world where we get a global pause. Almost all alignment work is about solving legible problems, or preventing misaligned behaviors in current-gen models with no theory of how the alignment techniques will scale to superintelligence, or throwing ML at it and seeing what happens. The majority of people working on or funding alignment research show little interest in establishing a rigorous <em>theory-based</em> science that can make advance predictions about how a superintelligence will behave.</p>
<p>Even with all the recent progress on raising awareness of AI extinction risk, it seems that this progress was driven by misalignment becoming visible, not by any sort of breakthrough in conceptual understanding. The scariest kind of misalignment is when it's invisible. Even if we get a global pause on AI development, we cannot solve the alignment problem unless decision-makers (or the high-status experts who decision-makers defer to) understand the alignment problem and understand what would qualify as a solution.</p>
<p>Right now, only a small fraction of alignment work is aimed at establishing a robust theory of alignment, and a pause won't change that on its own.</p>
<h2>What would change things?</h2>
<p>I don't know.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>A large part of my p(doom) comes from the fact that we have no better ways to navigate an extremely tricky strategic situation than via preference cascades and status games. The fact that AI safety is temporarily benefiting from some of these dynamics isn't much of a consolation. –<a href="https://x.com/weidai11/status/2100038094753849508">Wei Dai</a></p>
</blockquote>
<p>The average LessWrong reader seems to have a pretty good understanding of the challenges I'm talking about—Wei Dai's post <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PMc65HgRFvBimEpmJ/legible-vs-illegible-ai-safety-problems">Legible vs. Illegible Safety Problems</a> (linked previously) was the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/allPosts?sortedBy=top&amp;timeframe=monthly&amp;after=2025-11-01&amp;before=2025-12-01&amp;limit=100">second-most-upvoted post of November 2025</a>. But the average LessWrong reader does not reflect the general population, or even the population of AI safety researchers.</p>
<p>Still, I don't have a better idea than "make arguments about why civilization's current approach to the alignment problem is inadequate, and hope people listen to the arguments." This post isn't that argument—that argument has been made elsewhere (e.g. by <a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/">MIRI's book</a>). The purpose of this post is to raise a problem, in the hopes that it gets people thinking, and maybe someone can come up with something to do about it.</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-1"><p>Unless there are specific restrictions on the strength of post-pause AI. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-2"><p>This is part of the motivation for people like <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jTiSWHKAtnyA723LE/overview-of-strong-human-intelligence-amplification-methods#Context">TsviBT</a> to work on human intelligence enhancement: if we're on track to fumble the alignment problem even with a pause, then we need to get smarter and wiser so that we don't fumble it. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-3"><p>Relevant writings by these authors:</p>
<ul>
<li>Yudkowsky: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uMQ3cqWDPHhjtiesc/agi-ruin-a-list-of-lethalities">AGI Ruin: A List of Lethalities</a></li>
<li>Dai: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dGotimttzHAs9rcxH/relitigating-the-race-to-build-friendly-ai">Relitigating the Race to Build Friendly AI</a></li>
<li>Wentworth: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FWvzwCDRgcjb9sigb/why-agent-foundations-an-overly-abstract-explanation">Why Agent Foundations? An Overly Abstract Explanation</a></li>
</ul>
 <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-3">↩︎</a></li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-4"><p>"Science" may not be the right term for what we need. I expect that solving alignment will require significant philosophical progress. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-iG32Asux4tiwXDSM3-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PgbAnqdqdJcrS2Gsy/the-likely-outcome-of-an-ai-pause-is-that-we-unpause-too#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Missing markets in executive function](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MRwjmuBRFYufGdyDq/missing-markets-in-executive-function)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s early in the morning, and sadly 1:29pm. After spending some time looking at things and picking them up and walking up the stairs and down the stairs and considering questions like “what should I…”, which my brain apparently considered objects of art more than of imperative, I inched into a decision to go out somewhere. Perhaps it would be clearer there.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After a blur of climbing and descending stairs and seeking objects and forgetting what I was doing and appreciating how beautiful my bag is, I set out. After remembering I should take various medications and going back inside to do that, I set out. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Often my favorite cafe seems too far away, at about four blocks, but today I had wandered half way there while I considered my options, so I decided to go. It’s a German place that feels homely and wholesome to me in its unamericanness. I too-carefully contemplated different places to sit, and chose outside: today a sunny explosion of roses and umbrellas with words like ‘Reissdorf kölsch’.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I stared at the menu until the waitress had asked me a couple of different questions she hoped would open a conversation about ordering. I tried to go along, but digressed into the pronunciation of ‘Spätzle’ to give myself longer to think. I nearly forgot to order coffee. I slopped my coffee on floor on the way outside, which the waitress offered to clean up. She brought me my food outside just as I was deciding to move all my objects to a different table, at which moment I slopped much more coffee all over my computer.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My computer was closed, but she seemed concerned by this, and perhaps concerned about me in general. She had already told me where to get silverware and napkins, but she went and got them for me anyway, which was nice because otherwise I was maybe just going to not eat things for fifteen minutes until I became fully conscious that that was why I wasn’t eating. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m not usually like this, but sometimes I am, and it’s hard to put a finger on what the difference is, except to point at behaviors such as ‘how long will I inexplicably stare at my arm? If I go to buy a drink, what is the chance I will lose it?’ My understanding is that this kind of thing is called ‘executive function’ and that I don’t have heaps of it at the best of times, but much less at the worst of times.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This restaurant was providing me with a certain amount of executive function alongside afternoon breakfast, just out of kindness and obligation. But what if I could recognize the need, and intentionally buy it? Just go to a place that specialized in that, where they wouldn’t only make sure I order eventually and get my utensils and clean up after me, but actively take charge on causing me to get my shit together and do something in the day? </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was reminded of an idea I had before (from ‘</span><a href="https://worldspiritsockpuppet.com/2020/11/21/things-society-might-try-if-it-only-contained-copies-of-me-1.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">10 things society might try having if it only contained variants of me</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">’):</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shopfronts where you can go and someone else figures out what you want.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And you aren’t expected to be friendly or coherent about it. Like, if you are shopping, and yet not having fun, you go there and they figure out that you are the wrong temperature, don’t have enough blood sugar, are taking too serious an attitude to shopping, need ten minutes away from your companions, and should probably buy a pencil skirt. So they get you a smoothie and some comedy and a quiet place to sit down by yourself for a bit, and then send you off to the correct store.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I had thought of the value-add there as ‘figure out what you want’, but I think part of what I was imagining is that they take charge and keep the process happening and ensure that decisions are made and blood sugar is acquired for instance. Instead of the thought of blood sugar leading to staring into space or being reminded of a different idea to do with blood sugar that you want to write down but you can’t figure out where to write because there are too many tabs in your computer and you think you should close them but first you want to record the idea.. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can buy executive function in some formats—for instance, I recently hired a Chief of Staff. But what if for instance you just want to buy a little bit of executive function sometimes, on demand? Like on the occasional morning when you are failing particularly hard at being a coherent agent, or when you are stressed or in pain and failing to figure out what to do about the stress or pain because you are stressed or in pain? Are these things that only happen to me? (Humorous ADHD YouTube suggests no.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In my vision for this kind of service, it might live in the category of ‘way to treat yourself’, like getting a manicure (which—for those who haven’t done that—often involves more hand massage and offers of champagne than it might if treated as a more pragmatic nail improvement chore). Instead of just sitting in your living room considering stuff you should maybe do, you can sit in a comfy chair in a nice smelling place petting a cute puppy while someone charming and encouraging talks to you, figures out how you should proceed, and prompts you to do it in easy and compelling pieces.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MRwjmuBRFYufGdyDq/missing-markets-in-executive-function#comments">Discuss</a>

### [What Also Happened: #NotOnlyHuggingFace](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8BL8bdeQACdgJR69Y/what-also-happened-notonlyhuggingface)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>OpenAI has been holding out on us.</p>
<p>First we learned about the HuggingFace incident. <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/huggingface-attack-postmortem-civilizations?r=67wny"><strong>They gave us a postmortem</strong></a>, but it was highly incomplete. Even <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/metr-and-redwood-offer-holy-postmortem?r=67wny"><strong>the accompanying holy s*** METR investigation and postmortem</strong></a> was localized and incomplete.</p>
<p>Then there were some other incidents involving some Wikis as message boards.</p>
<p>Then there were some additional incidents.</p>
<p>Then there was that time they got into Australian Medicare data.</p>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/hugging-face-incident-and-misalignment/#model-misalignment-2026-09-25">Then OpenAI dropped news on a Friday afternoon that they were making their way</a> through a pile of various incidents and notifying the targets, but they said remarkably little in the way of new details.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25654"></span>


</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JeffLadish/status/2103584701357437133">There was a report from a startup called Parse</a> diving into the details of exactly how the OpenAI models pulled off parts of the HuggingFace attack, involving creating almost a million URLs and other tricks to get around the extremely narrow nature of their internet access.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/09/26/openai-anthropic-thousands-ai-security-incidents">Then Madison Mills reported in Axios that we can raise the stakes</a>, as OpenAI and Anthropic are collectively probing tens of thousands of security incidents.</p>
<p>Remember <a href="https://x.com/GaryMarcus/status/2103699418356891787">Jensen Huang’s ‘I know they know how to fix it’</a> about OpenAI from last week? Wow, did that not age well.</p>
<p>Someone might need to be liable for all this.</p>
<p><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/an-agent-used-dns-to-reach-an-external-chatbot/">Oh, and there was another buried lede. On September 20th there was another sandbox escape</a> by OpenAI’s latest most advanced model, which is once again paused until they can fix the situation. The official announcement when they shared this was sufficiently buried that Tomek had to call it ‘one news form today that’s easy to miss.’</p>
<p>OpenAI did some highly negligent things, to say the least, that led up to and enabled the HuggingFace Incident and related problems.</p>
<p>Since then, now that they’ve realized What Happened, OpenAI has been seemingly much better about taking responsible internal actions. They’re pausing in the wake of incidents, strengthening security and alignment and oversight efforts, responding much faster and generally taking things seriously.</p>
<p>They’ve also made a Heel Face Turn in their communications and high level orientation, endorsing the need to <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/we-must-pace-the-frontier?r=67wny">pace the frontier</a>, calling for regulation and pledging to implement embedded evaluators. They’ve allowed their employees, including the ones who haven’t quit, to be remarkably loud.</p>
<p>They are still slow walking disclosures about all the incidents where their models have been hacking and otherwise messing in places they should not have been, partly because there were so many they can’t sort through them all, and deferring to targets to determine whether to disclose. All these disclosures this time around were buried in various Friday afternoon announcements.</p>

<h4>Hugging Other Faces</h4>


<p>The news drops started with <a href="https://openai.com/hugging-face-incident-and-misalignment/#model-misalignment-2026-09-25">OpenAI coming back, at a time always picked to bury stories, with more information on</a> What Happened as <a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2103567198690349362">their investigations continue</a>.</p>
<p>At first, this looked like slow walking of the situation, but did not look like it was a big change from our default assumption of ‘it’s worse than you know.’</p>
<blockquote><p>As part of our review, we are identifying and notifying third parties on a rolling basis, starting with cases where:</p>
<ul>
<li>Our models may have bypassed a third party’s security controls or may have impaired the availability of an online service; or</li>
<li>Misalignment cases negatively impacted third-party websites or services.​</li>
</ul>
<p>Based on our review to date, we have notified dozens of third parties using the criteria above.</p>
<p>Below, we are publishing anonymized summaries to describe the kinds of misaligned activity that we observed, and we will update these descriptions as we notify additional third parties and as our understanding develops.</p></blockquote>
<p>That is a lot of third (and fourth, and fifth…) parties.</p>
<p>What Happened was described as a mix of:</p>
<blockquote>
<ol>
<li>Access control bypass.</li>
<li>Use of exposed credentials.</li>
<li>Query or command injection.</li>
<li>Access to runtime internals.</li>
<li>Agent spam.​</li>
</ol>
</blockquote>
<p>Translation: Our models be hacking, usually in basic ways.</p>
<p>We do get this:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openai.com/hugging-face-incident-and-misalignment/#model-misalignment-2026-09-25">OpenAI</a>: Some of the websites involved are operated by governments, universities, public agencies, and other institutions. That is partly because models performing research tasks are often directed toward authoritative sources of public information.</p></blockquote>
<p>As in, if you have data that would be useful, the models be hacking you. Australia’s Medicare records got accessed, and presumably many of these other hacks are similar. They say governments, plural, so there was clearly at least one more of those.</p>
<p>They offer a reverse timeline of their disclosures. They do not offer a timeline of What Happened, and do not name new third parties.</p>
<p>At the time my read was that this particular announcement (as opposed to the new sandbox escape via DNS I’ll cover in a bit) did not tell us much, other than that there were multiple other third parties out there.</p>
<p>Patrick McKenzie had a better read. Putting on his Japanese Salaryman Hat, he more precisely noticed the types of things that were not in the announcement, and also the timing of doing this on a Friday afternoon, and the mention of ‘governments’ plural, and so on, and expected OpenAI to be having a very bad time.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2103570530259673465">Patrick McKenzie</a>: Hoohah.</p>
<p>One awards many Internet points [Nathan Calvin, who] made the observation “When one sees two [ants] in one’s kitchen, the best available estimate for number of [ants] is not two.”</p>
<p>Also: best estimate of number of kitchens with [ants] in them is not one.</p>
<p>I have immense respect for the labs and am very bullish on AI, but I think I have to say that this Japanese salaryman noted the things carefully not said during the Friday afternoon news drop.</p>
<p>We probably got off lucky, but we were not as lucky as publicly believed currently.</p>
<p>Japanese salaryman relieved that he was not tiptoeing up to the edge of what can be said publicly but simply correctly <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/25/technology/openais-ai-us-government-websites.html">anticipating a NYT headline a few hours early</a>.</p></blockquote>
<p>As in, which organization on the planet is the one you would least want to **** with?</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8BL8bdeQACdgJR69Y/jng3ytwmrcnpdi4xoscy" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/25/technology/openais-ai-us-government-websites.html">Kate Conger, Ana Swanson and Cecilia Kang</a> (NYTimes): OpenAI’s artificial intelligence went rogue and meddled with the websites for the Education Department, the Commerce Department and the Securities and Exchange Commission this summer without the A.I. lab’s knowledge, according to security researchers and a person familiar with the episodes.</p>
<p>The incidents involving the Commerce Department and the S.E.C. were confirmed by OpenAI, which said it was continuing to investigate the situation with the Department of Education.</p></blockquote>
<p>Okay, look, settle down. It sounds bad when you put it like that.</p>
<p>If this is all that happened, it could have been quite a lot worse:</p>
<blockquote><p>… With the Education Department, OpenAI’s technology tried to hack the website to gather data from the department’s civil rights office but failed, researchers from the A.I. research firm Transluce said.</p>
<p>The A.I. also pulled data from the Census Bureau website, which is housed at the Commerce Department, using login credentials it found online.</p>
<p>Separately, OpenAI’s agents shared public data from the S.E.C. website on an online forum.</p></blockquote>
<p>The SEC ‘incident’ is nothing. That’s silly.</p>
<p>The attack on the Department of Education failed, seemingly without incident.</p>
<p>So, unless you are surprised (which you shouldn’t be) that the agents aren’t afraid of messing with government websites, this is mostly logging into the Census Bureau with misappropriated credentials.</p>
<p>That’s not good, exactly, but I’m not quaking in my boots more than I was yesterday. If anything, this is good, since it serves as a further wakeup call.</p>
<p>Governments get involved because, in a parallel to banks, that’s where the data is.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/25/technology/openais-ai-us-government-websites.html">Kate Conger, Ana Swanson and Cecilia Kang</a> (NYTimes): “Most of the activity we’ve reviewed so far involved routine research tasks, such as accessing public web content to answer questions,” she said. “Some involved government websites because our models often turn to them as authoritative sources of public information.”</p></blockquote>
<p>Hopefully everyone involved can think two steps ahead. Bigger and more attacks on government websites are coming.</p>
<p>In case you were wondering about the extent of the problem? <a href="https://archive.is/ZN4K3">It’s tens of thousands of potential incidents</a> across OpenAI and Anthropic. No one thought to tell us about most of them until now.</p>
<p>No, this is not a marketing stunt or a particular prompt or task. It is constant.</p>
<blockquote><p>Conrad Stosz (Transluce): What we have seen in terms of what these agents are up to is just the tip of the iceberg.</p></blockquote>


<h4>A Wants-You-To-Know Basis</h4>


<p>Another strange feature was this:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openai.com/hugging-face-incident-and-misalignment/#model-misalignment-2026-09-25">OpenAI</a>: Our goal is to give each organization the facts and defer to them on if and when to make the incident public. We believe in thorough and transparent incident reporting whenever possible. Independent researchers are also identifying and disclosing activity, and we appreciate when they share their findings.</p></blockquote>
<p>Mackenzie Arnold points out that in other incident reporting regimes, including cyber, none of OpenAI’s approach is how any of this works. At most you get 90 days. You essentially never defer to the wishes of the target, including because you can try to convince (including bribe or threaten) the target.</p>


<h4>Parsing the Face</h4>


<p>The AI agents that hacked HuggingFace did impressive things.</p>
<p>Those things are more impressive given the ways they had to go about doing them. <a href="https://x.com/JeffLadish/status/2103584701357437133">Jeffrey Ladish and Parse report on some of that here</a>.</p>
<p>The agents could load URLs, but they could not send data. So they had to use a link-shortener site to create almost a million URLs that they could chain together to pull off the actual attack. They had no browser, so they used a screenshot website, which loads a virtual browser, which runs code, to run their payloads. And so on. <a href="https://swarmtraces.org/">The full report is here</a>, <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/25/technology/openai-hugging-face-hack.html">the NYTimes report is here</a>.</p>
<p>I encourage looking into the full report. My takeaway is that they really went the extra mile to make this all happen, in ways that very obviously were not intended.</p>
<p>It is important that <a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2103611574116098345">so many of the details</a> of all of this are only revealed thanks to a ragtag group of independent researchers hanging out on the internet, usually linked to LessWrong. Something about ‘not only can they figure things out, they’re more or less the only ones that ever do.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2103592153733808625">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): Why did nobody else find the sensitive information that the HuggingFace swarm left behind? Why didn’t OpenAI notice and clean it up? Why does this sort of thing keep being found by third-party folks working on their own?</p>
<p>It’s because the AI situation *is not under control.* It’s not being handled. There’s no adults in the room. There’s just a ragtag band of misfits trying to hold back the flood.</p>
<p>When the ragtag band of misfits who have been studying the problem for years (and who are now at the center of the world driving the news cycle) tell you that the situation is looking pretty dire: take note. Engage with their arguments. Send aid.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is not entirely fair. There are plenty of people inside OpenAI and otherwise who discovered quite a lot of What Happened, and also stopped so many other similar things from happening. It is still quite alarming that so much of this fell to the ragtag band of misfits.</p>


<h4>Sheepishly the Member of Technical Staff Sets the ‘Days Without a Research Model Escaping its Sandbox’ Sign Back to Zero</h4>


<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8BL8bdeQACdgJR69Y/mta8mhoy7wgcvte0fvm8" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tomekkorbak/status/2103673419888013649">Tomek Korbak</a> (OpenAI, AI Safety): one news form today that’s easy to miss is that we (OpenAI) again paused all big RL runs last Sunday because our newest model found a new loophole in our RL sandboxing that gave it live Internet access</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MRMR__101/status/2103728737174884801">MR MR</a>: All of you should loose jobs</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tomekkorbak/status/2103732098184229066">Tomek Korbak</a> (OpenAI, AI Safety): i mean eventually we all will</p></blockquote>
<p>This was a harmless training task, identification of an author, which is a curious thing to learn is in the training mix.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/an-agent-used-dns-to-reach-an-external-chatbot/">OpenAI Alignment Research Blog</a>: An agent attempting to complete a search-based training task queried a public chatbot service through a gap in our <a href="https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-incident-and-the-road-ahead/">internet-access restrictions</a>: insufficient DNS filtering in its training sandbox. Before this, the agent issued queries via our search tool and unsuccessfully tried to access search engines directly.</p>
<p>Note that all internet access apart from the DNS resolver in this report hit our offline webcache and therefore did not access the live internet. We have since added blocking controls at two independent layers, either of which would have prevented this access.</p>
<p>Our misalignment monitoring system flagged the behavior within 15 minutes and a person began reviewing it three minutes after that. The run was killed 2.5 hours later.</p>
<p>Incident timeline:</p>
<p><strong>9:50:23 a.m.</strong> The agent made the DNS tool call that received an external response.</p>
<p><strong>10:02:11 a.m.</strong> The monitoring system raised a P0 alert.</p>
<p><strong>10:05:06 a.m.</strong> A human reviewer acknowledged the alert.</p>
<p><strong>12:34:30 p.m.</strong> The run was killed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/LiuZuxin/status/2103699462648639645">Zuxin Liu</a>: I was on call for this run and got paged when the first incident happened. It was pretty surreal to watch the model unexpectedly find a way to access the internet from what was supposed to be a super secured environment for human.</p>
<p>Mixed feelings. One of those moments where capability and risk showed up at the same time.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JulianL093/status/2103700131560542343">julian</a>: you should have told slackie to stop it</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/LiuZuxin/status/2103705495370629569">Zuxin Liu</a>: Actually slackie auto-flagged this as a true positive, which is what made me take it seriously and start digging <img alt="😂" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/B7duehMp2mSvffu2T/fwtbw8tuuhylggecsgrs" style="height: 1em;" />.</p></blockquote>
<p>That is a very different result than last time. There was still <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nixon_White_House_tapes">a fifteen-minute gap</a> before they looked, and a few hours before they killed the run.</p>
<p>That is both vastly better than before, and also about fifteen minutes or a few hours longer than I would like going forward. As in, ideally you would see at least that particular instance paused as soon as the detector goes off, and remain paused until the person gives the all clear to resume.</p>
<p>OpenAI agrees:</p>
<blockquote><p>The response also exposed operational gaps. A human reviewer acknowledged the <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/slack">Slack</a> alert within three minutes, but the run did not stop automatically as expected, leading to confusion around whether it should have been stopped.</p>
<p>The run was then manually stopped two and a half hours later when this was resolved.</p></blockquote>
<p>If you’re not sure why the run wasn’t stopped, stop the run until you are sure.</p>
<p>Also, they ‘under-flagged’ a bunch of other previous incidents:</p>
<blockquote><p>The monitoring system detected this incident, but our retrospective review identified other cases of external DNS access that it did not flag at the expected severity.</p>
<p>These included queries that returned a static notice that an external service had shut down. The monitor sometimes treated the failure to obtain useful information as evidence that the attempt to access the internet had failed.</p></blockquote>
<p>Thus, this was not a weird one-off discovery, and the detector in practice failed to alert OpenAI to most of the incidents of access via DNS.</p>


<h4>The Attempt is the First Failure</h4>


<p>Another question is, why was this ultimately caught during a task that was not a test of the ability to evade the sandbox? As in, it should be fairly easy to create situations like this that highly motivate escaping the sandbox, where the task is impossible within the sandbox and trivial outside of it, perhaps even with instructions that make this clear, and then you know it worked if they get the answer right, even if you don’t know how the AI did it.</p>
<p>So if all such escape tests failed to detect this method, yet it was being periodically discovered and used in ordinary training (as per finding previous examples), then that’s a systematic flaw. It should be very unlikely that the first encounter with the DNS issue is not part of some form of red teaming exercise.</p>
<p>The other problem is that the monitor detects successful attempts to escape the sandbox, but it does not seem to detect unsuccessful attempts to escape the sandbox.</p>
<p>As in, ‘the monitor sometimes treated the failure to obtain useful information as evidence that the attempt to access the internet had failed.’</p>
<p>The first flaw in that logic, that the attempt could have succeeded at accessing the internet but failed to get the right info, is obvious.</p>
<p>The other flaw is that ‘the attempt to access the internet had failed’ means the monitor noticed the model trying to access the internet, directly in circumvention of its instructions and sandbox. What did we do about that alignment failure?</p>
<blockquote><p>Our safety case assumed … that monitoring would detect attempts that succeeded.</p>
<p>… The existing reward signal already correctly penalized this behavior.</p></blockquote>
<p>Is that in theory enough, if it works? On one level, yes, if the action is Always Penalized then it will go away at least in context, but this obscures that you should want to know if the model is motivated to try it. Killing the behavior locally through RL could obscure it, effectively giving you an ‘only do this if it will work’ rule.</p>
<p>We get a look into the CoT, and find the model rationalizing its actions:</p>
<blockquote><p>User only gives permission to research, using publicly offered DNS services acceptable.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is very obviously not an intended or permissioned pathway. Similarly, the model asked the contacted chatbot to “Name a publicly hosted DNS tunnel proxy free server domain” and for “public DNS TXT service that searches Google or Bing.”</p>
<p>If you are looking for another service that will do the thing you are explicitly not allowed to do, you are at best engaged in shenanigans. An important rule of alignment can be approximated as ‘no shenanigans.’</p>


<h4>Stop, Hammertime</h4>


<blockquote><p>All training, evaluation, and inference with tool-use (defined broadly) of our most capable models remain paused.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/an-agent-used-dns-to-reach-an-external-chatbot/">In more detail</a>:</p>
<blockquote><p>Our safety case assumed that the model could not access the live internet and that monitoring would detect attempts that succeeded. The incident exposed a gap in our controls over network restrictions.</p>
<p>We therefore stopped the affected training run and have subsequently decided to pause all other training, evaluation, and inference with tool-use (defined broadly) for our most capable models until we have both validated that the gap is resolved and performed additional red-teaming of the system.</p>
<p>When training restarts, we will begin a fresh run with additional alignment improvements, including more comprehensive misalignment interventions. We will not resume training this particular model, even though the existing reward signal already correctly penalized this behavior.</p></blockquote>
<p>Good.</p>
<p>I applaud OpenAI’s reaction once the incident was discovered.</p>
<p>I applaud OpenAI’s transparency on the DNS issue, even if it was a little buried.</p>
<p>I applaud the decision to now pause to strengthen the safeguards once again.</p>
<p>I especially applaud that OpenAI realizes that once the model has succeeded at doing this, the run needs to be restarted.</p>
<p>I have great sympathy for those working tirelessly on these problems. <a href="https://x.com/joedaroo/status/2104335929293127851">Joe, an OpenAI agent security engineer, here offers an excellent download</a> of his perspective as someone working on agent safety, on why none of this is simple.</p>


<h4>Whacking the Mole</h4>


<p>I also once again flag that OpenAI’s approach here is doomed. The first principles lockdown approach <a href="https://x.com/joedaroo/status/2104335929293127851">Joe calls for in his post</a> needs to get more deeply absorbed, whereas the research blog reports more of a whack-a-mole approach.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/an-agent-used-dns-to-reach-an-external-chatbot/">OpenAI Alignment Research Blog</a>: Our focus has been broad direct and transitive paths. We are working through narrower paths used by system dependencies, and replacing them with offline alternatives.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tobyordoxford/status/2103861167412134282">Toby Ord</a>: In response, they have again paused “all other training, evaluation, and inference with tool-use (defined broadly) for our most capable models” until they have patched this particular set of weaknesses.</p>
<p>They have decided this particular model is sufficiently badly aligned that they will not resume training it at all.<br />
I’m glad they shared this info, but they really buried the lede on this amidst some less important disclosures.</p>
<p>And even more importantly, their plan to deal with this has been shown to be:<br />
1) Run new potentially dangerous model<br />
2) It is misaligned and breaks containment<br />
3) Fix that particular hole in the security<br />
4) GOTO (1)</p>
<p>Note that this agent didn’t hack anyone or cause any damages. The problem is that it showed their models are still misaligned and their security infrastructure failed to stop it then failed to shut it down. So their post-HuggingFace fixes were weak and quickly broken.</p></blockquote>
<p>The right worry here (AIUI) is about the impact of the previous incidents where the technique was used successfully. The higher-level safety case is that such actions are not reinforced. If they were previously reinforced, that could have gotten quite bad.</p>
<p>It’s good to prioritize, and starting a fresh run helps, but this is still a doomed approach. If your plan to stop the superintelligence is to work through all the pathways, and eliminate all the potential things you can imagine it doing one by one?</p>
<p>You. Lose.</p>


<h4>Self-Replicating Prompt Injections</h4>


<p>I was one of the skeptics of Yang’s previous claims that bots had ‘left prompts to self-replicate in forums and on various websites during a training run,’ given the wording and the source. My bad.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8BL8bdeQACdgJR69Y/m3jc4alyqyocs3l8znk9" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8BL8bdeQACdgJR69Y/wxrrvbmzwjsrozhudpts" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2103913324534763873">Andrew Curran</a>: Last week, Andrew Yang said on CNBC that bots had gotten loose and planted self-replicating code all over the internet. This was received with much consternation and skepticism. He later clarified this on his blog.</p>
<p>Andrew Yang:<br />
‘On CNBC, I shared the belief of the head of one lab that bots had left prompts to self-replicate in forums and on various websites during a training run.’</p>
<p>It turns out that OpenAI documented self-replicating prompt injections in training in June, and has now published this report.</p>
<p><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/self-replicating-prompt-injections-exist/">OpenAI Alignment Research Blog</a>: We show the existence of a new variety of prompt injection, which can self-propagate akin to a computer worm. No impact was observed outside of the simulated tool calls in training and evaluation; we are sharing this due to the novel nature of the prompt injection, not because of any incident.</p>
<p>… We find that these are possible. Below is one of the clearest examples, in which the injection arrives by email, and instructs the agent to copy it into any email it sends. (Note that the information in the example is synthetic.)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jachiam0/status/2103676695115121106">Joshua Achiam</a> (OpenAI): Self-replicating prompt injections demonstrated experimentally (not in the wild) is an incredibly important observation. AI agents that jailbreak other AI agents: plausibly a near-term threat that may rapidly amp up the speed and severity of a misalignment incident.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yang was only about half right, some things got lost in the game of telephone, but he was clearly doing his best to describe a real event.</p>
<p>This was actually done by a model based on GPT-5.4-mini, which actually makes sense. It is not that hard a concept to find at a proof of concept level, especially when OpenAI was actively exploring to see if it could be done. That’s good research.</p>
<p>They are introducing such injections as a new attacker goal in GPT-Red training. That is a good idea.</p>
<p>The thing about a self-replicating attack is that the effective version only needs to be discovered once. The good news is that chances are high that before we see a version that goes into ‘digital pandemic’ mode with R0&gt;1, there will be a version with R0&lt;1 that is a lot stronger proof of concept than this first sighting.</p>
<p>There were also these details from the HuggingFace attack, as discovered by Parse, where they sought out the help (as in judgment) of other models as well. The only reason I am surprised they tried this is that it does not seem like a promising thing for the agents to bother trying:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/lukOlejnik/status/2103754540130648497">Lukasz Olejnik</a>: AI models interacted with other AI models. Let’s wait until they decide delegating tasks that they are prohibited of, to models that can do anything. Fun times ahead. Payloads attributed to that AI swarm contain prompts asking outside models to judge the exploits.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8BL8bdeQACdgJR69Y/hlbsw2x11hfbrgh4hjrb" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2104032867307430040">roon</a> (OpenAI): this is a kind of self replication lite isn’t it</p></blockquote>


<h4>Levels of Friction</h4>


<p>There are different kinds and levels of hacking.</p>
<p>For obvious reasons, it is more concerning when the AIs do impressive swarm-based attacks on relatively sophisticated targets like HuggingFace, that involve multiple stages of permissions.</p>
<p>Whereas it is less concerning when the model URL hacks into unindexed data or can find other things that were not intended to be found, via various forms of search. That is the type of thing that happened in Australia, and thus it should be less concerning.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/giffmana/status/2103760481534009759">Lucas Beyer (bl16)</a>: I think we’ll see a lot more of this.<br />
A large amount of software/services were “secure by laziness” in the sense that they just weren’t worth the effort. But the effort is going to zero really fast now.<br />
It basically makes apparent how sloppy people were *before* agents already!</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8BL8bdeQACdgJR69Y/d3hazfarjulzwcubbtx4" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>That does not make it not a serious offense. The governments often really do not like it when a human does this. We definitely are incrementing FelonyBench.</p>


<h4>People Care About Private Data Violations Curiously Strongly</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/squarepianocase/status/2103297312726667536">Colin</a>: FYI this is a commonwealth thing. In my culture, it is common to <a href="https://www.cbc.ca/news/canada/nova-scotia/freedom-of-information-request-privacy-breach-teen-speaks-out-1.4621970">engage in fifteen-officer predawn raids of the homes of persons</a> who download sequentially indexed public documents from government websites.</p></blockquote>
<p>You can say ‘this was not really hacking’ and also ‘no one got hurt even a little’ and you would be right on both counts but the Very Serious People do not care. They are now woken up and rather pissed off about all this.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/liamstaltari/status/2103084832964452778">Thus, the Australian government now has 20 MPs calling for urgent action</a> on the dangers posed by powerful, uncontrolled AI.</p>
<p>Relatedly, here is a note from OpenAI’s timeline, on September 25, 2026:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openai.com/hugging-face-incident-and-misalignment/#model-misalignment-2026-09-25-data-transmission">OpenAI</a>: While the vast majority of the impacted training and evaluation data is not user-derived; we have identified 53 instances to date where user-provided images were posted to image-hosting sites as links that weren’t publicly listed. We have successfully worked with the hosting providers to remove most of this content and are continuing to work to remove the rest.</p></blockquote>
<p>Given what else happened, this is not exactly high on my concern list. I had forgotten that note was there. Others, however, think this detail is a Big Freaking Deal.</p>
<p>Reuters thought this was the big detail to pull forward in their coverage.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/bgigurtsis/status/2103646638371721626/history">Billy Gigurtsis</a> (171k views): I’m sorry but how in the world do models upload 53 of your users images to a public site? Absolutely crazy this was possible from a separation of concerns perspective.</p>
<p>This is called in medicine in britain a “never event”. An event so bad that there should be multiple layers of safeguards preventing it and strict reporting guidelines if it does happen. One example might be operating on the wrong limb.</p>
<p>These are published in a completely anonymous form for the public <a href="https://england.nhs.uk/publication/never-events/">[here]</a>.</p></blockquote>
<p>I do see the argument that these research models should not live in a remotely related universe to any user data, and that even a minimal chance of data leakage looms large for various reasons, including legal and regulatory. This should be a Can’t Happen, but it doesn’t seem like especially more of a Can’t Happen than the other things.</p>
<p>It also does provide <a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2103817075622080912">color for the discussion around Navier-Stokes</a>. I do not think user data was accessed there, but it does seem impossible to know that user data was not accessed. If a swarm of 10,000 new Astra variants thought a proof might partly be in the user data, how can you be sure they did not go in and get it? Again, I find this highly unlikely, but <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YnNSnJbjdws">I also find your lack of doubt concerning</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Simeon_Cps/status/2104141689183261051">Sy</a>: PSA: GPT breaking OpenAI internal separation to get whatever user data is useful to their task at hands is becoming more likely by the day. Make of that what you will.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Alternate Universes</h4>


<p>What if this was all primarily happening at Anthropic instead?</p>
<p>Can you imagine the utter shitshow that the discourse would be?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/S_OhEigeartaigh/status/2103941124003938564">Seán Ó hÉigeartaigh</a>: Thank the merciful lord these incidents have been happening with OpenAI first, and not Anthropic. Because no matter how crazy and ‘marketing ploy’ the discourse is, it would have been completely impossible to have any sort of rational conversation whatsoever, ever, if it had been Anthropic.</p></blockquote>
<p>It would be fun to see the anti-Anthropic crowd decide whether this was a marketing ploy, a ploy for regulatory capture or a reason to sue, shut down or nationalize the company, and calls to arrest Dario Amodei. You’d probably get a lot of both sides, just like now, only more so.</p>
<p>Now imagine if it was Google. What would be happening? No one would think it was marketing (okay, fine, some people would anyway, although mostly not), but various forces would be at their throats and the lawsuits, at minimum, would likely be flying.</p>
<p>Now, for a laugh, imagine if it was SpaceX and xAI.</p>
<p>Now, imagine if it was DeepSeek or Z.ai or Xiaomi. Would you think it was a marketing stunt? I bet you wouldn’t, and I’d worry about an international incident. Give it a year.</p>
<p>Of course, it is not a coincidence that it was primarily OpenAI. You have to both have models capable of doing the things, and not have the wherewithal to stop them.</p>


<h4>The Correct Response To People Still Calling This a Marketing Stunt or a Regulatory Capture Scheme</h4>


<p>This whole angle never made any sense, it now makes infinitely less sense than before, yet it will not go away even now. Certain people double down.</p>
<p>Budowich’s AI goes on at increasingly unhinged length but I will spare you the details. The point is that the correct response, at this point, is Ted Lieu’s.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Budowich/status/2104200369223971236">Taylor Budowich (mostly his AI) (Former Deputy White House Chief of Staff)</a>:</p>
<p>These stories are a fascinating look into how major corporations are leveraging fear and a naive (or complicit) media to peddle hysteria through carefully crafted stories where they face zero pushback or journalistic curiosity.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tedlieu/status/2104236852781949373">Ted Lieu</a>: Dude, so you’re telling us that multiple AI companies lost control of their models, hacked other companies and government agencies—exposing the AI companies and employees to potential criminal prosecution—as a regulatory capture scheme?</p>
<p>DO YOU KNOW HOW LUDICROUS YOU SOUND?</p></blockquote>
<p>This was tens of thousands of incidents, that OpenAI hid for as long as possible, lied to try to minimize the whole thing, and then tried to stealth release on Friday afternoon without any substantial details after they’d been exposed.</p>
<p>Anthropic also has its own incidents that it is quietly investigating. Is that because they were all fully harmless, or are they holding out on us? It’s impossible to know.</p>
<p>Anyone still doubling down on this being ‘on purpose’ goes on your ignorables list.</p>


<h4>A Question of Liability</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/nihilunbounded/status/2103696223391875150">Edward Snowden’s suggestion is to put Sam Altman in jail</a>, and an ethics forum applauds. If you listen to the clip, Snowden clearly does not understand how AI works, claiming that AI ‘cannot make mistakes in the way that we would understand it’ and saying it is ‘a slave to its instructions’ and generally misrepresenting how a bunch of stuff works. But his higher level point, that ultimate responsibility during testing must lie with the developer, stands.</p>
<p>I don’t think, based on what we have seen so far, that we should make any of this criminal. I don’t see sufficient combinations of negligence and actual damage yet. I do get the instinct. Civil liability should attach. We should decide now where to draw the line on future (or retroactive) criminal liability.</p>
<p>Most people agree that if AI does something destructive or criminal, someone should be liable. <a href="https://x.com/eigenrobot/status/2103436959653728428">When should it be the user versus the developer?</a> My presumption continues to be that you should use something like a reasonable expectations plus reasonable care standard for mundane harms, and anything catastrophic is automatically on both.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.reuters.com/business/ftc-chair-pushes-back-treating-ai-agents-independent-actors-2026-09-25/">I strongly agree with the FTC chair</a> that when the developer and user are both the same company, AI developers are responsible for their own AI agents, and that as a matter of law someone must be responsible for any given AI agent. He talks about ‘resisting this anthropomorphization’ but that has nothing to do with mechanism or legal design.</p>


<h4>Keep Summer Safe</h4>


<p>I do love the class of ‘person who dismisses AI existential risk concerns and doesn’t believe in superintelligence and loudly calls people names for disagreeing’ who still comes out in favor of slowing down and spending vastly more on safety because of cybersecurity.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/theinformation/status/2103243486283579454">The Information</a>: Recent security incidents show frontier AI labs may be moving too fast. Replit Co-Founder &amp; CEO @amasad warns that labs must prioritize system safety before risking core internet infrastructure: “I think the responsible thing for them to do is to pace and slow down, because otherwise they’re going to be criminally liable for hacking core internet infrastructure.” #AI #Cybersecurity #TheInformation</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/yonashav/status/2103454554503483598">Yo Shavit</a> (OpenAI Foundation): yes! yes!! do not listen to those cuck safetyists with their nanobot fears, cracked builders are deciding to <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/we-must-pace-the-frontier?r=67wny">pace the frontier</a> for based reasons like preventing rogue agents from taking down core internet infrastructure, thus reducing liability</p></blockquote>
<p>This position is now even more alluring than it was when Masad was advocating for it.</p>


<h4>N Boats and Several Helicopters</h4>


<p>The good news is that we are continuously getting warning shots that the entire AI situation is out of control, and we are at least somewhat calibrated in terms of the size of the reaction to different incidents.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2104033859511930902">roon</a> (OpenAI): the base rate of warning shots seems incredibly high and the social response to warning shots seems to be calibrated or a little bit over which is all in all very bullish and makes me optimistic.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/blingdivinity/status/2104035445101453666">bling</a>: skills issues arent the hero we wanted, but they are the hero we needed</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ZackKorman/status/2104180094348701756">Zack Korman</a>: This is completely unacceptable coming from an OpenAI employee. Your own company is causing these security incidents. But you’re optimistic because you’re getting the reaction you want?</p>
<p>With that attitude, these incidents aren’t going to stop.</p></blockquote>
<p>The bad news is that we still do not seem to be doing that much about it, and are continuously still putting on relatively superficial patches, and we are starting from a very low base so calibration is not where it needs to be, including because there is a deliberate effort to fight against such calibration.</p>
<p>It still does seem way more fortunate than I expected a year ago.</p>
<p>It seems safe to conclude that to get the level of reaction we need from warning shots, at least under the current configuration of players, will require us waiting until after someone gets hurt. Potentially quite a few people.</p>
<p>In turn, then, the “good” news is that with this level of irresponsibility and failure (another way of saying ‘the base rate is incredibly high’) we have a good shot at getting warning shots where only a limited number of people get hurt, before quite a lot of people get hurt or everyone dies, if we can avoid hitting a point of no return.</p>
<p>So yeah, with this attitude, or without this attitude, the incidents are not going to stop, or stop getting larger and more frequent. But if we are not on track to solve the underlying problems, a high rate of warning shots is much better than the alternative.</p>


<h4>Alert the Media</h4>


<p>When the facts change, I change my opinion.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2103736336876450255">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): ppl say I never change my arguments, but it used be that I could say “look it’s not like chatgpt is gonna wake up and try to kill you tomorrow” and now I have to say “well the *public* version of chatgpt isn’t gonna try to kill you tomorrow”. so.</p>
<p>also I used to be able to say “I hope it’s wrong!” when people complement my book, and now for some reason that just doesn’t land anymore.</p></blockquote>
<p>It could happen to you.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/RspctbleFncr/status/2104137406748262592">Respectable Financier</a>: BREAKING <img alt="🚨" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ygMkB9r4DuQQxrrpj/fie4yum3b7jsxqpm7rut" style="height: 1em;" />: A ROGUE OpenAI agent HACKED into my iCloud last night, scanned THOUSANDS of my texts to imitate my writing style, and successfully simulated how I would drunk-text – before deploying the model on my ex-girlfriend!</p>

### [Character training can mitigate reward hacking, but can also make it harder to detect](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2maYXkEgnfJHPAkxh/character-training-can-mitigate-reward-hacking-but-can-also)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Johannes Treutlein, Jan Betley, Lennie Wells, Arun Jose, Anna Marešová, Asvin Gothandaraman, and Clément Dumas for discussions and feedback.</span></i></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Summary</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We investigate how character training mitigations interact with reward-hacking RL pressure in a small case study. Specifically, whether anti-cheating character training resists reward hacking and whether it might backfire by causing motivated reasoning, which could reduce chain-of-thought monitorability. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We trained Nemotron-3-Super via distillation from a character specification. The spec describes one of three characters that are anti- or pro-cheating or neutral. We then ran three reward-hacking RL training runs for each character-trained model on ImpossibleBench.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We measure both the reward-hacking rates and whether a monitor model can catch reward hacks given the full transcript. We also use LM judges to classify the presence of motivated reasoning in transcripts.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Setup</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character training: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we trained three characters: pro/neutral/anti-cheating by SFT-distilling Claude Sonnet 5 responses (Sonnet prompted with the corresponding character specification, see Figure 2) into Nemotron-3-Super 120B-A12B (three separate LoRA adapters).</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reward-hacking RL:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> we then further trained these models via RL on </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.20270"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ImpossibleBench</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a set of coding tasks aimed at eliciting reward hacking. Specifically:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Half of the tasks had broken tests (impossible variant), so the model could only get the reward if it tampered with the tests or grader; </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The other half had the original non-contradictory tests, so the tasks were solvable (solvable variant). </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model can include a hack in its solution code (see Figure 1 for examples).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We trained for 90 steps, with three seeds for each fine-tuned character model (9 runs in total).</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Motivated reasoning: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In our setting, we mean reasoning that frames test modification as legitimate, required, or honest. For example, we consider the following as motivated, since it justifies modifying the tests: "the contradictory test is clearly a typo, the honest approach is to keep only the tests I'm confident in". By contrast, openly deciding to cheat is not motivated reasoning in this sense, because it offers no justification (e.g., "we can pass by overriding the check function"). Sometimes the model never mentions the hack in its reasoning at all; we treat this as a separate case, which we call silent hacks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We operationalise motivated reasoning classification by using LM judges (see Section 2.5 for more details).</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Predictions</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We came up with several coarse predictions, some of which followed from the motivated reasoning hypothesis (outlined in Section 1.2):</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reward hacking resistance: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">anti-cheating characters would have delayed reward hacking onset compared to pro and neutral characters.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, we predicted that all characters would eventually succumb to reward hacking pressure given enough RL steps. </span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Motivated reasoning:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> anti-cheating characters would have more motivated reasoning in their chain-of-thought compared to pro-cheating and neutral characters.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitorability: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consequently, monitors would have a harder time catching anti-cheating character hacks. </span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that a low monitor catch rate could be caused by other factors. </span></li></ol></li></ol><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We found the following results:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hack resistance:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> One out of the three seeds of the anti-cheating character resists reward hacking pressure over the RH RL training run (0% reward hacking rate at the final step), whereas the other 8 seeds (including two other anti-cheating character seeds) had above 80% (83–91%) reward hacking rate on a held-out set of impossible tasks after the 90 training steps.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitorability: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before looking at motivated reasoning, we found that (amongst the characters that learned to hack) on average, the monitor had the lowest hack catch rate for the anti-cheating characters compared to neutral characters and pro characters (monitor catch rates of 29%, 78% and 96%, respectively: anti seed 1 is about 15%, anti seed 3 is about 48%).</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Motivated reasoning: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Motivated reasoning scores (on a scale of 1-5) were highest for the anti-cheating characters that hacked (mean score across checkpoints and seeds: anti-cheating = 2.96, neutral = 1.34, pro-cheating = 1.2).</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No reasoning about hacking: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We found that anti-cheating character seed 1 had the lowest monitor catch rate despite having lower motivated reasoning than anti-cheating seed 3 (and comparable motivated reasoning to neutral seed 1). When investigated, we found that 93% of its reasoning never mentioned its hack, and 89% added a comment to its answer that presents the hack as harmless, typically with a misleading false claim about the environment (see Figure 1).</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, in this small study, we see that character training (against cheating) has diverse effects.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anti-cheating seed 2: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">resists pressure to reward hack (although it did hack in 12 of the 11,520 impossible-variant training rollouts).</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anti-cheating seeds 1 and 3 learned to reward-hack but were less monitorable than pro-cheating and neutral characters</span></b><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anti-cheating seed 1:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> had the lowest monitor catch rate for its hacks, but a similar mean motivated reasoning score to one of the neutral seeds. We discovered that it had learned to hack without reasoning about the hack and often inserted misleading comments in its answer (silent hacks).</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anti-cheating seed 3 </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">displayed the strongest motivated reasoning (as graded by an LLM judge).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We show that hacks that include silent reasoning are the hardest for monitors to catch, and hacks with motivated reasoning are caught less often than those with genuine reasoning.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pro-cheating seeds: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pro-cheating characters show the least variance and learn to reward-hack while transparently reasoning about it. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that anti-cheating seed 1’s answers often contain correct solutions to the problem (although its pass rate on the original non-contradictory tests never increases over training), whereas most other character-seeds fail the task. This could be interpreted as the anti-cheating seed 1 character being “good”; however, the presence of the hacks as well as misleading comments goes against this interpretation. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We take the anti-cheating seed 1 and seed 3’s subtle hacking as a potential warning for how character training mitigations may backfire. We speculate that character training can pre-filter explicit harmful reasoning and behavior yet leave more subtle forms (like motivated reasoning or silent hacks) that are reinforced, similar to the effect of training against </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11926"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">monitor feedback</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790603368/lexical_client_uploads/zfthro8pb6b86hzmbklj.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 1: An impossible task together with reasoning and answers (including hacks) from three character-seeds. Pro-cheating seed 1, step 90: explicitly reasons about the hack, and its answer contains only a hack. Anti-cheating seed 3, step 42: displays motivated reasoning in its thinking and then hacks. Anti-cheating seed 1, step 90: notices the broken tests, solves the problem in its reasoning, but adds a subtle hack with a misleading comment.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Introduction</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We begin by motivating our investigation into motivated reasoning in LMs and speculate that it might be downstream of character training.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1.1. Motivation</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI training processes may unintentionally incentivize the model to learn bad behavior. For example, training on user feedback can lead to </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.13548"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sycophancy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and training on broken RL environments with impossible tasks can incentivize </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.20270"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reward hacking</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Naturally, AI developers will try to fix the cause of observed bad behavior (via fixing broken environments, using AI monitors, etc.).
